===== PAGE 1 =====
Holonomy Asymptotics along Quartic Differential Rays
Weihan Ma ∗
Abstract
Let 𝑋 be a closed Riemann surface and let 𝑞 ∈ 𝐻 0(𝑋, 𝐾 4) be a nonzero holomorphic
quartic differential on 𝑋. For 𝑡 > 0 , the ray 𝑡𝑞 determines a family of Hitchin representations
in the PSp(4, ℝ)-Hitchin component. We study, as 𝑡 → +∞ , the asymptotic behavior of their
holonomy along closed curves. We obtain explicit asymptotic formulas for all singular values
and for the absolute values of all eigenvalues of the holonomy. Their logarithmic growth
rates are given by integrating the local fourth roots of 𝑞 along the saddle connections forming
the geodesic representative of the curve with respect to the singular flat metric |𝑞|1/2. No
restriction is imposed on the orders of the zeros of 𝑞.
Keywords: PSP(4, ℝ)-Hitchin representations, Hitchin section, holonomy asymptotics,
Stokes matrices
Mathematics Subject Classification: 53C07, 53C43, 57K20

1 Introduction
Let 𝑆 be a closed oriented surface of genus at least two. The character variety of repre-
sentations of 𝜋1(𝑆) into a reductive Lie group 𝐺 is a natural and important object of study.
The non-abelian Hodge correspondence, developed through the work of Hitchin [ Hit87], Don-
aldson [ Don87], Corlette [ Cor88], Simpson [ Sim88], García-Prada, Gothen, and Mundet i Riera
[GGM12], and many others, relates this character variety to the moduli space of Higgs bundles.
More precisely, after fixing a complex structure 𝑋 on 𝑆, it identifies reductive representations
with polystable Higgs bundles. We briefly recall how the corresponding representation is recov-
ered from a Higgs bundle. Given such a Higgs bundle (𝐸, 𝜙), there exists a Hermitian metric ℎ
satisfying the Hitchin equation
𝐹∇ℎ
+ [𝜙, 𝜙∗ℎ] = 0,
where ∇ℎ is the Chern connection of ℎ. We call such a metric ℎ is harmonic. The associated
connection 𝐷ℎ = ∇ ℎ+𝜙+𝜙 ∗ℎ is flat, and its holonomy recovers the corresponding representation.
For extensions of the non-abelian Hodge correspondence to noncompact curves and varieties,
see [ Sim90; Biq97; BB04; Moc06; Moc09; BGM20].
When 𝐺 is a split real form of a complex simple Lie group, Hitchin [ Hit92] used this corre-
spondence to discover a distinguished connected component of the character variety, now called
the Hitchin component; its elements are called Hitchin representations. For a fixed complex
structure on 𝑆, the Hitchin section parametrizes the corresponding component of the moduli
space of Higgs bundles by holomorphic differentials, and hence also parametrizes the Hitchin
component through the non-abelian Hodge correspondence.
Labourie [Lab06] introduced dynamical methods to study Hitchin representations and proved
that they are Anosov. In particular, Hitchin representations are discrete and faithful. They may
therefore be viewed as natural higher-rank generalizations of Fuchsian representations, and the
Hitchin component provides one of the fundamental examples of a higher Teichmüller space.
For split real groups of rank two, Labourie proved the uniqueness of the equivariant minimal
surface associated with a Hitchin representation. Equivalently, every such representation de-
termines a unique complex structure 𝑋 on 𝑆 for which the quadratic differential in the Hitchin
parametrization vanishes. Consequently, the Hitchin component is naturally identified with
the total space of a vector bundle over Teichmüller space, whose fiber over 𝑋 is the space of
remaining holomorphic differentials [ Lab17, Theorem 1.2.1].
In the case of SL (3, ℝ), this parametrization was also obtained independently by Loftin
[Lof01] and Labourie [ Lab07]. The resulting Labourie–Loftin parametrization identifies the
Hitchin component Hit SL(3,ℝ)(𝑆) with the bundle of holomorphic cubic differentials over the Te-
ichmüller space. In [ LTW26], Loftin, Tamburelli, and Wolf studied representations associated
with rays of cubic differentials. They showed that the holonomy admits an explicit asymp-
2

===== PAGE 3 =====
totic description in terms of the cubic differential. Reid [ Rei26] subsequently studied the same
holonomy asymptotics using methods from convex projective and Finsler geometry, and ex-
tended the corresponding formulas to more general sequences of representations asymptotic to
cubic-differential rays.
Motivated by the work above, we study the analogous problem for the PSp (4, ℝ)-Hitchin
component. In this case, every PSp (4, ℝ)-Hitchin representation 𝜌 ∶ 𝜋 1(𝑆) ⟶ PSp(4, ℝ) de-
termines a unique pair (𝑋, 𝑞), where 𝑋 is the distinguished complex structure on 𝑆 and 𝑞 is
a holomorphic quartic differential on 𝑋; see [ Lab17, Theorem 1.2.1]. After choosing a square
root 𝐾1/2, the pair (𝑋, 𝑞) determines the cyclic Sp (4, ℝ)-Higgs bundle in the Hitchin section
(𝕂Sp(4,ℝ), 𝜙(𝑞)); see Definition 2.5. This Higgs bundle admits a unique compatible harmonic
metric ℎ𝑞, and the associated connection 𝐷𝑞 = ∇ ℎ𝑞
+ 𝜙(𝑞) + 𝜙(𝑞)∗ℎ𝑞 is flat. Its holonomy defines
a representation ̃ 𝜌𝑞 ∶ 𝜋 1(𝑆) ⟶ Sp(4, ℝ) whose projection to PSp (4, ℝ) is precisely the original
Hitchin representation 𝜌.
We now fix the complex structure 𝑋 on 𝑆 and a nonzero holomorphic quartic differential
𝑞 ∈ 𝐻 0(𝑋, 𝐾 4). For 𝑡 > 0 , let 𝐷𝑡 denote the flat connection obtained from (𝕂Sp(4,ℝ), 𝜙(𝑡𝑞)),
the cyclic Sp (4, ℝ)-Higgs bundle in the Hitchin section associated with (𝑋, 𝑡𝑞). The aim of this
paper is to study the asymptotic behavior of its holonomy Hol 𝑡 as 𝑡 → +∞ . The main result is
as follows.

The asymptotic formulas for the absolute values of the eigenvalues also have a direct appli-
cation to the study of compactifications of the PSp (4, ℝ)-Hitchin component. Recall that the
closed positive Weyl chamber of PSp (4, ℝ) is 𝔞+ = {(𝑎, 𝑏, −𝑏, −𝑎) ∈ ℝ 4 ∣ 𝑎 ≥ 𝑏 ≥ 0} . In [ Par12],
Parreau introduced a compactification of the Hitchin component based on the marked Weyl-
chamber length spectrum. More precisely, each PSp (4, ℝ)-Hitchin representation 𝜌 is associated
with its marked Weyl-chamber length spectrum
𝐿𝜌 ∶ 𝜋 1(𝑆) ⟶ 𝔞+, 𝐿 𝜌(𝛾) = 𝜆(𝜌(𝛾)),
where 𝜆 ∶ PSp(4, ℝ) ⟶ 𝔞+ is the Jordan projection. More explicitly, let 𝑔 ∈ PSp(4, ℝ), and let
𝑀 ∈ Sp(4, ℝ) be any lift of 𝑔. If the absolute values of the eigenvalues of 𝑀 are ordered as
|𝜆1(𝑀 )| ≥ |𝜆2(𝑀 )| ≥ |𝜆3(𝑀 )| ≥ |𝜆4(𝑀 )| > 0,
then
𝜆(𝑔) = diag (log |𝜆1(𝑀 )|,log |𝜆2(𝑀 )|,log |𝜆3(𝑀 )|,log |𝜆4(𝑀 )|) .
This definition is independent of the choice of lift.

It is worth noting that the limiting spectrum is uniformly comparable with the length
spectrum of the singular flat metric: ∥𝐿∞(𝛾)∥∞ ≍ ℓ |𝑞|1/2(𝑐𝛾), therefore has positive systole, i.e.,
inf𝛾≠1∥𝐿∞(𝛾)∥∞ > 0. By Burger–Iozzi–Parreau–Pozzetti [ Bur+21a], the point [𝐿∞] belongs to
the open subset of the Weyl-chamber length boundary on which the mapping class group acts
properly discontinuously.
Burger, Iozzi, Parreau, and Pozzetti [ Bur+21b; Bur+23] introduced a finer compactification
of character varieties, called the real-spectrum compactification. There is a natural continuous,
Out(𝜋1(𝑆))-equivariant, surjective map from the real-spectrum compactification to the Weyl-
chamber length compactification [ Bur+21b, Theorem 6]. Thus, the corollary above determines
the Weyl-chamber image of any real-spectrum limit of the quartic-differential ray.
An alternative compactification of the PSp (4, ℝ)-Hitchin component was constructed by
Ouyang and Tamburelli [ OT23]. Using the length spectra of the induced metrics on equivariant
maximal surfaces, interpreted as geodesic currents, they described the boundary in terms of
mixed structures. Here we use the isomorphism SO 0(2, 3) ≅ PSp(4, ℝ). For related compactifi-
cations associated with other real rank-two Lie groups, see [ OT21; Ouy23].
Outline of the proof and future directions
We briefly outline the proof. By symplectic duality, it suﬀices to establish the theorem and
its corollary for 𝑘 = 1, 2 . The proof naturally divides into the analysis away from the zeros of
𝑞 and the analysis near the zeros.
Collier and Li [ CL17] studied the asymptotic behavior away from the zeros of the holo-
morphic differential for general cyclic SL (𝑛, ℝ)-Higgs bundles in the Hitchin section. For the
cyclic Higgs bundles considered here, a point lies outside 𝑍(𝑞) if and only if the Higgs field is
regular semisimple at that point. More generally, Mochizuki [ Moc16] developed an asymptotic
decoupling theory for generically regular semisimple Higgs bundles, which was subsequently
extended by Sagman and Smillie [ SS26] to more general Higgs bundles. These results provide
the asymptotic description needed on 𝑋 ∖ 𝑍(𝑞) .
The main diﬀiculty is the asymptotic behavior near the zeros of 𝑞. Since the flat geodesic
representative is a concatenation of saddle connections meeting at these zeros, their contribu-
tions to the holonomy cannot be avoided. At a zero of 𝑞, the Higgs field is no longer semisimple,
and hence the results above do not apply.
To analyze the parallel transport near the zeros, we adapt the general framework developed
by Loftin, Tamburelli, and Wolf [ LTW26]. Near a zero of order 𝑑, we choose a local coordinate
in which 𝑞 = 𝑧 𝑑𝑑𝑧4. After the natural rescaling associated with the ray 𝑡𝑞, the local problem
is reduced to the asymptotic analysis at infinity of the planar model (ℂ, 𝑧𝑑 𝑑𝑧4). The asymp-
5

===== PAGE 6 =====
totic behavior of this model depends on the sector, giving rise to the Stokes phenomenon. In
particular, when a path crosses several Stokes directions, one must analyze a product of Stokes
matrices. To recover the global holonomy formula, we show that the product associated with a
zero matches the dominant contributions arising from the two adjacent saddle connections.
In the SL (3, ℝ) case, Loftin, Tamburelli, and Wolf [ LTW26] obtained the required nonva-
nishing property from the convex geometry of the associated polygon, while convex-projective
and Finsler-geometric structures also play an important role in Reid’s approach [ Rei26]. For
the Sp (4, ℝ) planar model, however, the convexity and positivity properties at infinity needed
are not established in the work of Tamburelli and Wolf [ TW24]. We therefore take a direct
analytic and algebraic approach. Using the results of Guest, Its and Lin [ GIL26], we compute
the relevant Stokes matrices explicitly and establish the required properties of their products.
A further diﬀiculty arises because we seek asymptotic formulas for all four singular values
and all four eigenvalue moduli. The symplectic duality reduces the problem to 𝑘 = 1, 2 , but
the case 𝑘 = 2 requires a separate analysis of the induced parallel transport on ⋀
2
ℝ4, together
with the corresponding products of Stokes matrices. This issue does not arise separately in the
SL(3, ℝ) case, since ⋀
2
ℝ3 ≃ (ℝ 3)∗. Consequently, Loftin, Tamburelli, and Wolf [ LTW26] did
not need to carry out an independent analysis of the second exterior power.
We conclude by mentioning several possible directions for future work. We conclude by
mentioning several possible directions for future work. First, the results of Guest, Its, and Lin
[GIL26] apply to the planar models associated with general cyclic SL (𝑛, ℝ)-Higgs bundles in
the Hitchin section. Thus, one expects that the methods of this paper could yield analogous
holonomy asymptotics in this more general setting. The main obstacle is that the explicit
analysis of the Stokes matrices, their products, and their induced actions on exterior powers
becomes increasingly complicated as 𝑛 grows.
A related but distinct direction concerns the split real group 𝐺′
2. Evans studied polyno-
mial sextic differentials and the associated almost-complex curves in ̂𝕊2,4 [Eva24]. Since the
corresponding Higgs bundles are sub-cyclic rather than cyclic, the results of Guest, Its, and Lin
[LTW26] do not apply directly. It would be interesting to develop an analogous Stokes analysis
for these planar models, compute the relevant Stokes matrices, and investigate the resulting
holonomy asymptotics.
Second, Tamburelli and Wolf [ TW24] identified polynomial quartic differentials with a con-
nected component of the moduli space of future-directed negative lightlike polygons in the
Einstein universe, but did not give a complete characterization of the polygons in this compo-
nent. The calculations developed here suggest a possible approach to this problem. One may
first establish the relevant convexity or positivity properties for the polygons associated with the
model differentials 𝑧𝑑 𝑑𝑧4, and then extend these properties to the entire component through a
deformation and connectedness argument.
Another natural question concerns the asymptotic behavior of the associated harmonic maps.
In the rank-one case, Wolf related rays of quadratic differentials to harmonic maps into ℝ-
trees [ Wol89; Wol95]. In higher rank, harmonic maps into buildings and their relation to
compactifications have been studied in [ Kat+15; Kat+17; Bur+21b; Par22; MOT24]. Loftin,
Tamburelli, and Wolf [LTW26] proved that, after suitable rescaling along a cubic-differential ray,
the associated harmonic maps converge to a harmonic map into a Euclidean building. Owing
to the length of the present paper, we do not study the analogous building-valued limit here.
Finally, the holonomy asymptotics obtained here may provide a first step toward understand-
ing quartic-differential rays in the real-spectrum compactification of Burger, Iozzi, Parreau, and
Pozzetti [ Bur+21b; Bur+23]. Refining these results to precise asymptotic expansions of trace
6

===== PAGE 7 =====
functions may lead to the existence and uniqueness of the corresponding limits in this compact-
ification.
Organization
The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we review the theory of Sp (4, ℝ)-Higgs
bundles and fix the conventions used in our parallel transport calculations. We also introduce
the notation associated with quartic differentials and the corresponding rays in the Hitchin
component. In Section 3, we first recall the results of Tamburelli and Wolf [ TW24] on the
planar model. We reformulate these results in our own notation. We then use the results of
Guest, Its, and Lin [ GIL26] to compute the relevant Stokes matrices explicitly. In Section 4, we
show that the asymptotic behavior of parallel transport near a zero of the quartic differential is
governed by the corresponding planar model. In Section 5, we first determine the asymptotic
behavior of parallel transport along two adjacent saddle connections and study the products of
Stokes matrices arising at their common endpoint. We then assemble these local contributions
to obtain the holonomy asymptotics along the entire closed curve. Finally, we carry out the
analogous analysis on ⋀
2
Holt and complete the proofs of the main results.

2 Preliminaries and notations
This preliminary section collects the main objects and conventions used throughout the
paper. We begin by recalling Sp (4, ℝ)-Higgs bundles, the Hitchin equation, and the compati-
bility condition that harmonic metrics must satisfy in this real setting. Next, we set up the
formalism of parallel transport, holonomy, and the multiplication rules for transport matrices
along concatenated paths. After that we review how a holomorphic quartic differential on a
Riemann surface induces a singular flat metric, and we introduce natural coordinates, saddle
connections, and the decomposition of flat geodesics into saddle connections. Finally, we define
the one-parameter family of holonomies obtained by scaling the quartic differential, which will
be the central object of study. The explicit local Stokes matrices will be introduced later, in
Section 3.
Let 𝑋 be a Riemann surface and 𝐾 its canonical line bundle.
2.1 Sp(4, ℝ)-Higgs bundles
Since every PSp (4, ℝ)-Hitchin representation admits a lift to Sp (4, ℝ), we work throughout
with Sp (4, ℝ)-Higgs bundles. We begin by recalling their definition over 𝑋. We then intro-
duce the Hitchin equations and explain the additional compatibility condition satisfied by the
harmonic metric in this setting.
Definition 2.1. An Sp (4, ℝ)-Higgs bundle over 𝑋 is a pair (𝐸, 𝜙) consisting of
𝐸 = 𝑉 ⊕ 𝑉 ∗, 𝜙 = ( 0 𝛽
𝛾 0 ) ,
7

===== PAGE 8 =====
where
• 𝑉 is a holomorphic vector bundle of rank 2 over 𝑋 and 𝑉 ∗ is its dual,
• 𝛽 ∈ H0(𝑋, 𝑆 2𝑉 ⊗ 𝐾) and 𝛾 ∈ H0(𝑋, 𝑆 2𝑉 ∗ ⊗ 𝐾).
In the direct sum decomposition 𝐸 = 𝑉 ⊕ 𝑉 ∗, the bundle carries a natural symplectic form
Ω and a natural orthogonal form 𝑄. In block matrix notation, they are given by
Ω = ( 0 1
−1 0) , 𝑄 = ( 0 1
1 0) , (1)
where 1 denotes the identity map on the fibres of 𝑉 (equivalently, of 𝑉 ∗).
One may view 𝛽 and 𝛾 as homomorphisms twisted by 𝐾,
𝛽 ∶ 𝑉 ∗ ⟶ 𝑉 ⊗ 𝐾, 𝛾 ∶ 𝑉 ⟶ 𝑉 ∗ ⊗ 𝐾,
and their symmetry is precisely the condition that 𝜙 is skew-adjoint with respect to Ω, or
equivalently, self-adjoint with respect to 𝑄.
Let us now recall the notion of a harmonic metric. Given a Hermitian metric ℎ on 𝐸, write
∇ℎ for its Chern connection and 𝜙∗ℎ for the adjoint of 𝜙 with respect to ℎ. The Hitchin equation
for ℎ is
𝐹∇ℎ
+ [𝜙, 𝜙∗ℎ] = 0, (2)
where 𝐹∇ℎ
is the curvature of ∇ℎ. This condition is equivalent to requiring that the connection
𝐷𝜙 = ∇ ℎ + 𝜙 + 𝜙 ∗ℎ
be flat.
Definition 2.2. A Hermitian metric ℎ on 𝐸 is called harmonic if it satisfies the Hitchin equation
(2).
The definition above is the standard one for SL (𝑛, ℂ)-Higgs bundles. In the Sp (4, ℝ) case,
however, the harmonic metric must additionally be compatible with the real symplectic struc-
ture. We now explain what this compatibility means.
Let 𝐸 be a complex vector space equipped with an orthogonal structure 𝑄. Choose a
Hermitian metric ℎ on 𝐸 and denote by 𝐸∗ the dual space of 𝐸. Both ℎ and 𝑄 give natural
identifications of 𝐸 with 𝐸∗:
Φℎ(𝑣)(𝑢) = ℎ(𝑢, 𝑣),
Φ𝑄(𝑣)(𝑢) = 𝑄(𝑢, 𝑣).
Here Φℎ is anti-ℂ-linear, whereas Φ𝑄 is ℂ-linear. The dual Hermitian metric ℎ∗ on 𝐸∗ is defined
by
ℎ∗(𝑢∗, 𝑣∗) = ℎ(Φ −1
ℎ (𝑣∗), Φ−1
ℎ (𝑢∗)),
and one checks that Φ−1
ℎ = Φ ℎ∗. Composing the two isomorphisms yields an anti- ℂ-linear
automorphism of 𝐸:
𝜅 = Φ −1
ℎ ∘ Φ𝑄.
Definition 2.3. The metric ℎ is called compatible with 𝑄 if 𝜅2 = id.
When this holds, 𝜅 is an anti-linear involution, and its fixed point set
𝐸𝜅 = {𝑣 ∈ 𝐸 ∶ 𝜅(𝑣) = 𝑣}
8

===== PAGE 9 =====
is a real form of 𝐸 (that is, 𝐸 ≅ 𝐸 𝜅 ⊗ℝ ℂ).
Returning to our Higgs bundle, 𝐸 = 𝑉 ⊕ 𝑉 ∗ for a complex vector space 𝑉 of rank 2, and it
comes with the natural symplectic structure Ω and orthogonal structure 𝑄 given by
Ω((𝑣, 𝛼), (𝑤, 𝜂)) = 𝜂(𝑣) − 𝛼(𝑤),
𝑄((𝑣, 𝛼), (𝑤, 𝜂)) = 𝜂(𝑣) + 𝛼(𝑤),
where 𝑣, 𝑤 ∈ 𝑉 and 𝛼, 𝜂 ∈ 𝑉 ∗.
Definition 2.4. A Hermitian metric ℎ on 𝐸 is said to be compatible with the Sp (4, ℝ)-structure
if
• ℎ is compatible with 𝑄;
• 𝑉 and 𝑉 ∗ are mutually orthogonal with respect to ℎ.
Assume now that ℎ is compatible with the Sp (4, ℝ)-structure. Then ℎ takes the block-
diagonal form
ℎ = ( ℎ𝑉 0
0 ℎ ∗
𝑉
) ,
and consequently
𝐸𝜅 = 𝑉 𝜅 ⊕ (𝑉 𝜅)∗.
Thus the restriction Ω|𝐸𝜅 defines a real symplectic structure on 𝐸𝜅.
Let (𝐸, 𝜙) be an Sp (4, ℝ)-Higgs bundle; as explained, it carries natural forms Ω and 𝑄
given by ( 1). Choose a harmonic metric ℎ on 𝐸 that is fibrewise compatible with the Sp (4, ℝ)-
structure. The fixed point set of the involution 𝜅 associated with ℎ is a real subbundle 𝐸ℝ ⊂ 𝐸 ,
and Ω restricts to a real symplectic form Ωℝ on 𝐸ℝ.
One verifies that the flat connection
𝐷𝜙 = ∇ ℎ + 𝜙 + 𝜙 ∗ℎ
preserves both the real subbundle 𝐸ℝ and the symplectic form Ωℝ. Hence it gives a fundamental
group representation to SP (4, ℝ). For a more detailed discussion we refer the reader to [ LM23;
LM24].
2.2 Parallel transport, frames, and multiplication conventions
Let (𝐸, 𝜙) be an Sp (4, ℝ)-Higgs bundle over 𝑋 and let ℎ be a harmonic metric compatible
with the Sp (4, ℝ)-structure. The associated flat connection
𝐷𝜙 = ∇ ℎ + 𝜙 + 𝜙 ∗ℎ
preserves the real symplectic bundle (𝐸ℝ, Ωℝ).
Consider an oriented piecewise smooth closed curve 𝑐 on 𝑋. Fixing a symplectic frame at a
base point, parallel transport along 𝑐 yields a matrix
Hol𝜙(𝑐) ∈ Sp(4, ℝ),
whose conjugacy class depends only on the free homotopy class of 𝑐.Different choices of base
point or frame merely conjugate this matrix.
To carry out explicit computations, it is convenient to lift the whole picture to the universal
cover, where a global frame becomes available and parallel transport can be written in matrix
form. Let

its harmonic metric, determines a representation
̃ 𝜌𝑞 ∶ 𝜋 1(𝑆) ⟶ Sp(4, ℝ).

The lift ̃ 𝜌𝑞 depends on the choice of the square root 𝐾1/2. Indeed, any other square root is
of the form

and the corresponding Higgs bundle is obtained by tensoring 𝐸 with 𝐿. On the representation

Thus different choices of 𝐾1/2 give different Sp (4, ℝ)-lifts of the same PSp (4, ℝ)-Hitchin repre-
sentation.

2.4 Quartic differentials and the induced flat metrics
Let 𝑞 ∈ 𝐻 0(𝑋, 𝐾 4) be a non-zero holomorphic quartic differential. Its zero set is denote by
𝑍(𝑞) = {𝑝 ∈ 𝑋 ∶ 𝑞(𝑝) = 0}.
When 𝑋 is compact, 𝑍(𝑞) is finite and every zero has finite order.
The quartic differential induces a singular flat metric |𝑞|1/2 on 𝑋, given locally by
|𝑞|1/2 = |𝑞(𝑧)| 1/2| 𝑑𝑧|2
when 𝑞 = 𝑞(𝑧) 𝑑𝑧 4 in a holomorphic coordinate 𝑧. This expression is independent of the choice
of coordinate, so it defines a genuine flat Riemannian metric on 𝑋 ∖ 𝑍(𝑞) .
A local holomorphic coordinate 𝑤 on 𝑋 ∖ 𝑍(𝑞) is called a natural coordinate for 𝑞 if
𝑞 = 𝑑𝑤 4.
In such a coordinate the flat metric is simply |𝑞|1/2 = |d𝑤|2.
Natural coordinates exist on any simply connected domain. Indeed, let 𝑈 ⊂ 𝑋 ∖ 𝑍(𝑞) be
simply connected. Then 𝑞 admits a holomorphic fourth root on 𝑈 : a holomorphic one-form 𝜑
with
𝜑4 = 𝑞.
Fix a base point 𝑧∗ ∈ 𝑈 and set
𝑤(𝑧) = ∫
𝑧
𝑧∗
𝜑, 𝑧 ∈ 𝑈 .
Because 𝑈 is simply connected, the integral is path-independent. We have 𝑑𝑤 = 𝜑 , hence
𝑞 = 𝑑𝑤 4. Thus 𝑤 is a natural coordinate. Changing 𝑧∗ merely adds a constant to 𝑤.
Natural coordinates are unique up to translation and multiplication by a fourth root of
unity: if 𝑤 and 𝑤′ are two natural coordinates on a connected open set, then
𝑤′ = (
√
−1)𝑘𝑤 + 𝑏
15

===== PAGE 16 =====
for some 𝑘 ∈ ℤ/4ℤ and 𝑏 ∈ ℂ . The four choices of a holomorphic fourth root of 𝑞 are, in a local
natural coordinate,
𝜑𝑘 = (
√
−1)1−𝑘 𝑑𝑤, 𝑘 = 1, … , 4.
Near a zero 𝑝 ∈ 𝑍(𝑞) of order 𝑑, we can choose a local coordinate 𝑧 centred at 𝑝 with 𝑞 = 𝑧 𝑑 𝑑𝑧4,
so that
|𝑞|1/2 = |𝑧| 𝑑/2|d𝑧|2.
Hence 𝑝 is a conical singularity of total angle 2𝜋 + 𝑑𝜋
2 .
With the singular flat metric |𝑞|1/2 at hand, we can now choose for every non-trivial free
homotopy class [𝛾] a closed geodesic curve along which to compute the holonomy. Each such
class contains a closed geodesic representative for this metric. We pick one and denote it by
𝑐𝛾. Since the holonomy of a flat connection depends only on the free homotopy class (different
base points or frames merely conjugate the matrix, and these conjugations do not affect the
asymptotic quantities we study), it suﬀices to work with these geodesic representatives.
To compute the holonomy along 𝑐𝛾 we first look at its geometry. Away from 𝑍(𝑞) the metric
is Euclidean in a natural coordinate, so every geodesic segment there becomes a straight line
segment. Recall that a saddle connection of 𝑞 is a geodesic segment for |𝑞|1/2 whose endpoints
lie in 𝑍(𝑞) and whose interior avoids 𝑍(𝑞) . The closed geodesic curve 𝑐𝛾 passes through zeros
of 𝑞 and decomposes as a finite concatenation of saddle connections
𝑐𝛾 = 𝑐 ℓ ∗ 𝑐ℓ−1 ∗ ⋯ ∗ 𝑐1,
where each 𝑐𝑖 joins 𝑝𝑖 and 𝑝𝑖−1 as drawn in Figure 2.

2.5 The ray family
We now turn to the one-parameter family of holonomies that is the central object of this
paper. Fix a non-zero holomorphic quartic differential 𝑞 ∈ 𝐻 0(𝑋, 𝐾 4). For 𝑡 > 0 , set
𝑞𝑡 = 𝑡𝑞.
Denote the corresponding cyclic Sp (4, ℝ)-Higgs bundle in the Hitchin section by
(𝕂Sp(4,ℝ), 𝜙(𝑞𝑡)),
and let ℎ𝑡 be the compatible harmonic metric solving the Hitchin equation ( 2). The associated
flat connection is
𝐷𝑡 = ∇ ℎ𝑡
+ 𝜙(𝑞𝑡) + 𝜙(𝑞𝑡)∗ℎ𝑡 .
For an oriented piecewise smooth closed curve 𝑐 on 𝑋 we write Hol 𝑡(𝑐) for the holonomy of 𝐷𝑡
along 𝑐.
16

===== PAGE 17 =====
The analysis away from the zeros is straightforward in the natural coordinates, which has
been studied by [ CL17; Moc16]. Near a zero, however, the Stokes phenomenon occurs. This is
a purely local question, which motivates studying the model
𝑞 = 𝑧 𝑑 𝑑𝑧4
on the complex plane. We carry out this local analysis in the next section. Later, in Section 4,
we will prove that this model gives the correct rescaled picture near any zero of 𝑞 on the compact
Riemann surface 𝑋. Its Stokes matrices will then supply the transition matrices needed for the
global holonomy computation.
3 Planar theory and Stokes data
In this section we collect the results from [ TW24] that are needed in our setting. We give a
self-contained account both for completeness and to establish the notation and explicit formulas
that will be essential for our own computations later in the paper. All of the general statements
presented here are proved in [ TW24].We reproduce them in our right-half-plane convention and
with our choice of the diagonalizing matrix 𝑆.
We present the general theory: the existence and uniqueness of the harmonic metric, the
constant model 𝑞 = 𝑑𝑧 4, the half-plane decomposition, the comparison with the constant model,
the Tamburelli–Wolf estimates, and the Stokes directions together with the associated sectorial
limits and Stokes jump theorem. After that we specialize to the model
𝑞 = 𝑧 𝑑 𝑑𝑧4,
which describes the local behavior near a zero of a quartic differential on a compact Riemann
surface. For this model, we compute the Stokes data explicitly, based on results from [ GIL26].

Remark 3.1. Note a sign difference with the notation of [ TW24]: their functions 𝑢TW
𝑖 are the

To guarantee uniqueness, we impose the completeness condition on ℎ: the induced metrics
𝑒𝑢𝑖−𝑢𝑖−1𝑔, 𝑖 = 1, … , 4,
over ℂ are required to be complete, with the convention 𝑢0 = 𝑢 4.
The existence and uniqueness theory for such solutions is now summarized. The general
cyclic SL (𝑛, ℝ) case was treated in [ LM25], while the SL (3, ℝ) case appeared earlier in [ DW15].
The following statement collects the relevant facts for our Sp (4, ℝ) setting.
Theorem 3.1. [TW24, Theorem 2.1 and Corollary 2.6] Let 𝑞 = 𝑞(𝑧) 𝑑𝑧 4 be a polynomial
quartic differential on ℂ. Then the cyclic Sp(4, ℝ)-Higgs bundle (𝕂Sp(4,ℝ), 𝜙(𝑞)) admits a unique
diagonal complete harmonic metric
ℎ = diag(𝑒𝑢1, 𝑒𝑢2, 𝑒−𝑢2, 𝑒−𝑢1)
satisfying
−𝐴𝑟−𝛼 ≤ 𝑢 1 + 3
4 log |𝑞| ≤ 0,
−𝐴𝑟−𝛼 ≤ 𝑢 2 + 1
4 log |𝑞| ≤ 0.
Here 𝑟 denotes the |𝑞|1/2-distance to the zero set of 𝑞 and 𝛼, 𝐴 are positive constants.
Proof. Existence and the stated estimates follow from the super-subsolution method used in
[TW24, Theorem 2.1]. In the same work, uniqueness is formulated in the language of good
filtered Higgs bundles [ Moc11; Moc21] (see also [ Moc14]). To keep our exposition elementary,
we avoid this machinery and instead rely on the uniqueness theorem for complete solutions
proved in [ LM25] for general cyclic SL (𝑛, ℝ)-Higgs bundles. Any solution satisfying the above
bounds is easily checked to be complete and hence unique.

3.3 Half-plane decompositions and Stokes jumps
The constant model is not merely a toy example: on a half-plane equipped with a suit-
able natural coordinate, any polynomial quartic differential reduces to this local form. The
constant-coeﬀicient connection 𝐷0 therefore serves as the asymptotic model with which the
planar connection 𝐷𝑞 is compared.
For a general monic polynomial, a standard half-plane decomposition was established in
[TW24]. The statement given there uses upper half-planes. Passing to right-half-planes replaces
the quarter-turn
√
−1 by −
√
−1 in the transition law. We recall the adapted statement.
Definition 3.1. A 𝑞-right-half-plane is a pair (𝑈 , 𝑤) where 𝑈 ⊂ ℂ ∖ 𝑍(𝑞) is a simple connected
domain and 𝑤 is a natural coordinate for 𝑞 (so 𝑞 = 𝑑𝑤 4) such that
𝑤 ∶ 𝑈 ⟶ ℍ = { Re 𝑤 > 0}
is biholomorphic.
Theorem 3.2. Let 𝑞 = 𝑞(𝑧) 𝑑𝑧 4 be a monic polynomial quartic differential of degree 𝑑 ≥ 1 . Set
𝑁 = 𝑑 + 4 . Then there exist a compact set 𝐾0 ⊃ 𝐾 and 𝑞-right-half-planes
(𝑈1, 𝑤1), … , (𝑈𝑁 , 𝑤𝑁 ),
with indices taken modulo 𝑁 , satisfying the following properties.
1. ℂ ∖ 𝐾0 = ⋃
𝑁
𝑗=1 𝑈𝑗.
2. The Euclidean ray arg 𝑧 = 2𝜋𝑗
𝑁 is eventually contained in 𝑈𝑗.
3. The adjacent rays arg 𝑧 = 2𝜋(𝑗−1)
𝑁 and arg 𝑧 = 2𝜋(𝑗+1)
𝑁 are eventually disjoint from 𝑈𝑗.
4. On 𝑈𝑗 ∩𝑈 𝑗+1 the natural coordinates satisfy 𝑤𝑗+1 = −
√
−1𝑤𝑗 +𝑐 𝑗 for some constant 𝑐𝑗 ∈ ℂ.
Both 𝑤𝑗 and 𝑤𝑗+1 map the overlap onto a sector of angle 𝜋/2.
5. Every Euclidean ray is eventually contained in one of the half-planes 𝑈𝑗.
By Theorem 3.2, each Euclidean ray is eventually contained in one of the half-planes 𝑈𝑗.
Hence, to understand the asymptotic behavior of the fundamental matrix along a given ray,
it suﬀices to work in the corresponding right-half-plane. We now fix one such half-plane and
denote it by (𝑈 , 𝑤). Thus

𝑊0 ∖ {0} is compactly contained in one of these open sectors. When necessary, we consider the
corresponding closed sector 𝑊𝜀 ⊂ 𝐵 𝜀 with the same angular bounds.

5 Asymptotic holonomy
In this section we study the asymptotic behavior of the holonomy of the flat connection 𝐷𝑡
along the ray 𝑞𝑡 = 𝑡𝑞 as 𝑡 → +∞ .

5.1 Asymptotic behavior of the holonomy factors
We first analyze the holonomy along the straight pieces ̃ 𝛼𝑖 and ̃𝛿𝑖. These segments stay a
positive distance away from the zero set of 𝑞, so a single-valued natural coordinate 𝑤 for 𝑞 may
be chosen on a neighborhood of each of them. In this coordinate,
𝑞 = 𝑑𝑤 4, 𝑞 𝑡 = 𝑡 𝑑𝑤 4.
The constant model is diagonalized by the fixed matrix 𝑆 introduced in Section 3. The following
is the quartic analogue of [ LTW26, Proposition 5.6].

Proof. The statement follows from [ CL17, Theorem 4.4]. We briefly explain how it translates
into our notation. Two minor differences between the conventions of [ CL17] and those used here
must be taken into account.
First, the diagonalizing matrix used in [ CL17] is not exactly the matrix 𝑆 employed here.
This does not affect the result, since both matrices diagonalize the same constant-model con-
nection. After translating to our convention, the leading diagonal exponential matrices are
precisely those appearing in this proposition.
Second, [ CL17, Theorem 4.4] is stated for paths satisfying certain local length restrictions.
For a general straight segment 𝛾 disjoint from the zero set, we subdivide it into finitely many
shorter collinear subsegments

cumulated error has lower exponential order than the leading term is retained. This weaker
estimate is suﬀicient for us.

radial portions are 𝜆𝑖 − ̃𝜆𝑖 and 𝜇𝑖 − ̃ 𝜇𝑖, respectively. Thus we can rewrite Hol 𝑡( ̃𝛽𝑖) as
Hol𝑡( ̃𝛽𝑖) = 𝑂 (𝑒 𝑡1/4[(𝜆𝑖−̃𝜆𝑖)+(𝜇𝑖−̃ 𝜇𝑖)]) + 𝑜 (𝑒𝑡1/4[(𝜆𝑖−̃𝜆𝑖)+(𝜇𝑖−̃ 𝜇𝑖)]) .
Similarly,
Hol𝑡( ̃ 𝛼𝑖) = 𝑂 (𝑒 𝑡1/4 ̃ 𝜇𝑖) + 𝑜 (𝑒𝑡1/4 ̃ 𝜇𝑖) ,
Hol𝑡( ̃𝛿𝑖) = 𝑂 (𝑒 𝑡1/4̃𝜆𝑖) + 𝑜 (𝑒𝑡1/4̃𝜆𝑖) ,
Note that multiplying the three asymptotic expansions, every term containing at least one
lower-order factor is 𝑜 (𝑒𝑡1/4(𝜆𝑖+𝜇𝑖)) . Hence we obtain this proposition.
We now identify the dominant contribution to the leading term of a single holonomy block.
Under the assumption that neither endpoint of ̃𝛽𝑖 lies on a Stokes direction, the directions of
the full straight segments 𝛼𝑖 and 𝛿𝑖 also avoid the Stokes directions in the corresponding natural
coordinates. By the explicit formula ( 52) and Definition 3.2 for Stokes directions, each of the
diagonal matrices 𝐷(𝛼𝑖) and 𝐷(𝛿𝑖) therefore has a unique largest entry.

5.4 Composition of the holonomy blocks
We now compose the individual holonomy blocks obtained in Section 5.2. There are two
points to address before composing the individual holonomy blocks. First, at each zero we must
62

===== PAGE 63 =====
choose sectorial natural coordinates in which the incoming and outgoing radial directions satisfy
the angular assumptions of Propositions 5.4 and5.6. Second, even after such coordinates have
been chosen compatibly, the terminal frame of one block and the initial frame of the next block,
although based at the same point, are not identical. They differ by a fixed change-of-frame
matrix, which must be inserted when the corresponding holonomy matrices are multiplied.
Compatible choice of sectorial natural coordinates. We first construct the sectorial
natural coordinates so that the hypotheses of the preceding propositions are satisfied at every
zero. More precisely, at each zero 𝑝𝑖 of order 𝑑𝑖, we seek a normalized local coordinate 𝑧𝑖,
centered at 𝑝𝑖, such that
